Craft library. How anime is written at a world-class level, built source by source, tested against Hood Ninjaz.

Start here if you are writing tonight. Find your principle in the first table, open it, read the header block.

What binds writing. Nothing here binds a script until Bupe ratifies it into The Writing Lawsrepo. A principle's Standing field says which it is.

The rest. Rules and templates: PROTOCOL.md. Intake queue: ROADMAP.md. History: LOG.md.

P-001 Iconic powers

A power is iconic when one image tells you whose it is, so it is built from a familiar idea made strange or from one repeatable signature.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

A power reads as iconic when a single frozen frame is enough to name the character holding it, with no face, no costume and no line of dialogue in shot. That frame gets earned one of two ways: take a power the audience already knows and give it one twist nobody else has used, or give the power one repeatable signature moment that looks the same every time and belongs to nobody else. A power that does neither is competent, never memorable, no matter how strong it is on paper.

Why it works

An audience builds identity out of pattern recognition, not out of a stat sheet. The twist route works because familiarity plus one clean deviation is easier to hold in memory than a wholly new idea explained from scratch. The signature-move route works because repetition is what makes a beat readable at a glance: once a viewer has seen it land the same way twice, the third time needs no explanation at all. Both routes are cheap to stage and expensive to imitate, which is exactly the asymmetry a long-running story needs from its most-repeated visual.

How to apply it

  1. Before naming a power, write down the one thing a reader would draw from memory to prove they had seen it before, and cut anything that does not serve that one thing.
  2. If the base idea is common (fire, strength, speed, blades), find the single specific twist that could only belong to this character, and remove every other twist competing for the same slot.
  3. Design one repeatable visual or behavioural beat that fires identically every time the power activates, and hold it unchanged from its first appearance to any later reveal built on it.
  4. Test the power against a different character doing the same job with a different toolkit; if nothing on the page needs to change, the power is interchangeable rather than iconic.

The test

  1. Could a reader identify this character from one panel of the power alone, with no name and no face shown?
  2. Is there one thing this power does or looks like that no other character in the cast is allowed to do or look like?
  3. If the power is a familiar type, does this character's specific use of it differ from every generic version of that type?
  4. Does the power have a repeatable beat, or does it look different every time it is drawn?

Failure mode

The power reads as competent rather than memorable: a reader could swap it for a same-tier power belonging to a different character and nothing about the scene would need to change. A cast built this way blurs in silhouette, because nothing about any single power is doing identification work.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict
S-00100:25-01:35A familiar power (elastic stretching) reads as iconic because the specific, improvised use of it is unlike every other version of the same power typesupports
S-00101:36-02:36A wholly fresh power reads as iconic through a visually distinct look, and a power can also become iconic purely through a named, repeatable signature movesupports
S-00102:37-03:44A protagonist assembled from many other characters' ability types, with no one thing unique to him, reads as forgettable rather than iconicsupports (counter-example)
Jujutsu Kaisen Fandom-Confirms the two signature moves the source names exist as described (Kamehameha, Gojo's Unlimited Void); the craft inference is S-001's, not this source'ssupports

Disagreements

None yet.

P-002 Simple powers

One mechanism used cleverly beats a toolbox chosen from, because an audience can only feel tension when it already knows what the power can do.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

A protagonist's power should be one mechanism the character learns to use in ever more creative ways, not a growing shelf of separate abilities picked to fit each new problem. Tension comes from the audience already knowing the mechanism's limits before the scene starts; a fight becomes a puzzle the mechanism must solve, rather than a menu the character selects from. Side characters and villains may carry a wider kit, because the audience is not asked to track their internal logic scene to scene the way it tracks the lead's.

Why it works

If a reader already understands what a power can and cannot do, every new use of it is legible as either clever or reckless, and the writer can generate surprise from application rather than from inventing a new rule on the spot. A toolbox protagonist removes that legibility: any problem can be solved by reaching for whichever tool the scene needs, so no fight can ever really threaten the character, because the audience cannot predict what is off the table. One mechanism, applied differently each time, is also cheaper to plan across a long-running story: every future problem is already answerable by the same handful of rules the audience learned on day one.

How to apply it

  1. Write the power's mechanism as a single sentence a stranger could repeat back after one scene; if the sentence needs "and" to hold every ability, the power is a kit, not a mechanism.
  2. Before writing a fight, list the specific properties the mechanism has (what it changes, what it cannot change, what it costs) and solve the scene using only that list.
  3. When a scene seems to need a new ability, first ask whether the existing mechanism can be applied in an unfamiliar way instead of adding a new one.
  4. Reserve a genuine multi-ability kit for a side character or a villain, where the audience is not expected to predict every option before it is used.

The test

  1. Can the power be described in one sentence without an "and"?
  2. When this character solves a problem, does the solution use the same underlying mechanism as every other scene, applied differently?
  3. If a new ability shows up mid-story with no earlier hint of it, is there a stated reason the mechanism now does more than it used to?
  4. Could a reader predict, before the fight starts, roughly what tools this character has available?

Failure mode

The fight loses tension because the character's option list can grow to match whatever the plot needs; a reader stops asking "how will they solve this" because the honest answer is "with whichever ability the scene requires." The mechanism stops being a character trait and becomes a plot convenience.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict
S-00103:46-06:44One elastic mechanism, reapplied against very different opponents, beats a shelf of separately named abilities used one at a timesupports
S-00103:46-06:44A single transformation rule, reinterpreted per object, is simple even though its outputs vary widelysupports
S-00103:46-06:44A protagonist choosing from many separately learned abilities, rather than reapplying one, reads as less inventive even in the same fight structuresupports (counter-example)

Disagreements

None yet.

  • Jaden Young AT-RISK. See A-002.

  • Donte Jakes PASSES. See A-002.
  • If this section and an applied page disagree, the applied page wins and this one gets fixed.

P-003 Earned progression

A power-up is only satisfying when the audience has watched the bill being paid, in time, training, sacrifice or death.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

A power-up should land on the audience as a receipt for something already shown being spent: time passing, training endured, a sacrifice made, or a near-death survived. The size of the jump matters less than whether the audience watched the cost accrue first. A power-up with no visible cost reads as an unlock rather than a moment, because nothing on screen earned it.

Why it works

Satisfaction from a power-up is really relief that a debt has been paid, so the writer's job is to make the debt visible well before the payment. A jump that arrives with no shown cost reads as arbitrary, because the audience has nothing to measure the new strength against; the character was simply handed more. A jump that arrives after visible cost reframes the whole preceding stretch as investment rather than filler, which is why the strongest version of this beat lands hundreds of chapters, not scenes, after the story started asking for it.

How to apply it

  1. Before writing a power-up, decide what the character gives up to get it (time, a training relationship, a physical toll, a piece of themselves) and put that cost on the page before the payoff.
  2. Space power-ups apart deliberately; a jump that follows the last one too closely reads as an unlock rather than a threshold crossed.
  3. Land the power-up on an emotional peak the story has already been building toward, never on a schedule or a level marker.
  4. If a character's growth has no visible unit (an internal capacity rather than a number), design one external, visual readout for it before the audience is asked to track it.

The test

  1. Has the audience seen the character pay something specific before this power-up lands?
  2. Would a reader, looking back, be able to name the moment training or sacrifice began?
  3. Does the power-up land on an emotional beat the story has been building toward, rather than on a convenient scene?
  4. If the growth being tracked is internal, does the audience have any visible way to read it before the power-up arrives?
  5. Set beside a rival power-up on a much shorter clock, does this one still read as the slower, harder-won climb, or would a viewer struggle to tell the two apart on pacing alone?

Failure mode

The character's growth reads as a skip rather than a climb: an audience that has not watched the cost accrue receives the new power as an announcement rather than an earned moment, and the story's remaining threats feel arbitrary because there was no visible floor the character rose from.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict
S-00106:45-10:15A power-up landing on one of a story's most emotional moments, hundreds of chapters after the story began asking for it, reads as satisfying because the audience watched the cost accrue in fullsupports
S-00106:45-10:15Starting a character far below every other cast member and then compressing an enormous power climb into very few episodes reads as unsatisfying, because the audience is told the journey happened rather than shown itsupports (counter-example)
S-00208:34-10:56Deku's power-up cost accrues on screen across his fight against Todoroki, where he repeatedly breaks his own finger to force a shockwave, worsening the damage to his arm each time, before he slowly learns to control the power without destroying his bodysupports
S-00210:57-11:18Set beside Sung Jin-Woo, who reaches his peak on the short road, Deku's climb runs the long one; the source argues this shape, not the exact length, is why Deku's peak reads as earned and the rival's does notsupports
S-00205:32-08:34Sung Jin-Woo wins one major fight after another without visibly paying anything, so no single power-up in the sequence reads as a bill being settled, only as another free upgradesupports (counter-example)

Disagreements

None yet.

P-004 Flaws that never go away

A drawback that can be solved was a delay, not a flaw: the cost may change as the character grows, but the power is never without one, and the limits that stay are what make a win mean anything.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

Every power needs an active cost, though the cost does not have to be the same one forever: as a character grows, the specific limit they started with can be resolved and retired, so long as a new limit takes its place at the new tier of power. A limit that is resolved and never replaced was only ever a delay dressed as a flaw, not a real one. Whatever limit is currently in force is what makes a victory cost something and a loss survivable rather than certain; without one in force, every fight's outcome is decided by how much the plot needs the character to win.

Why it works

A cost the audience watches being paid holds tension for as long as it stays in force: every fight staged while it is live carries real stakes, because the character has not simply been handed a way around it. What drains that tension is a cost retired with nothing replacing it, since a limit gone for good teaches the audience to wait out every future limit rather than respect it. The best versions of this rule keep a cost in force at every tier, so the audience can never fully relax into assuming the character wins, no matter how strong that character becomes elsewhere, and the cheap, unlimited option is always sitting right there and always the wrong one to take.

How to apply it

  1. Name at least one cost of using the power that is active right now; it does not have to be the cost the power started with, but the power must never be free at its current tier.
  2. Make sure the cheap version of the power (the one with no limit) is always technically available, so choosing the limited version is a real decision, not a forced one.
  3. Give the current limit a visible, on-screen consequence at least once, rather than leaving it as a stated rule nobody has paid.
  4. Pick which cost type the power draws on, and let it show on screen: the body (the use itself does physical damage, as with Rock Lee's Eight Gates), the ability's own limits (a crude or incomplete version of the power rather than its full form, as with Yuji's Divergent Fist), or the mind (a cost paid in restraint and judgement, as with Ichigo's inner Hollow); a power may draw on more than one.
  5. When a character grows stronger and an old cost is retired, hand it a new cost at the new tier rather than letting the power reach a free ceiling: run the rotation the way One Piece's Gears retire one cost for the next, or the way Naruto's later forms each carry their own separate price.

The test

  1. Is there at least one cost of this power that is active right now, whether or not it is the cost the power started with?
  2. Is the cheap, unlimited version of the power always available, so restraint is a choice rather than a rule?
  3. Has the audience seen this limit actually cost the character something on screen, not just heard it described?
  4. Can you name which cost type is paying for this power: the body, the ability's own limits, or the mind?
  5. After the character's next power-up or form change, does a new cost take over, rather than the old one simply disappearing?

Failure mode

The character accumulates power with nothing currently at stake, so every fight's tension depends entirely on the plot choosing to make the character struggle; a viewer stops fearing for the character and starts merely watching, because the power itself no longer has a price. The clearest version of this symptom is a run of fights the character wins without taking any damage at all: every ability just works, nothing about using it costs anything any more, and each new fight reads as a formality rather than a real question.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict

S-00212:23-14:20Each new form carries its own distinct cost, and while some early costs ease as the character grows, the source argues there is always a limitation left in forcesupports

P-005 A group of individuals

A villain group is one force made of people you could each pick out of a line-up, and a member you have no screen time to make a person weakens the group; a faceless collective is a deliberate choice, never a default.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

A villain organisation has to read as one force and as a room full of separate people at the same time. The force comes from a purpose the audience could state in a sentence after one scene. The people come from each member carrying something that is theirs alone, a want, a method, an idea they embody, so that nobody in the group is merely the other one. That second half is bought with screen time and nothing else, which means the roster is priced by the time available: every member added past that point arrives as a name rather than a person, and spends credibility the developed members earned. The exception is deliberate and rare. Where the group's job in the story is scale, a tide rather than a set of antagonists, individuality is the wrong tool, and giving each member a face would shrink the very thing that makes them frightening. Decide which of the two you are writing before you name a single member, because the two designs pull in opposite directions and a group caught between them reads as neither.

Why it works

An audience cannot hold a roster, it holds people, so the organisation survives in memory only through whichever members the story managed to turn into people. A shared purpose does the unifying work cheaply, in a line, but distinctness has to be paid for in scenes, and there are only so many scenes. Roster size is therefore a budget question: divide the time available by the number of members and the answer is how much person each one can be. Past the point where that division stops producing people, extra members do not add threat, they dilute it, because the audience begins filing each new arrival as more of the same and stops expecting anything from the next one. The faceless collective works for the opposite reason: when the intended feeling is scale, sameness is the effect, and the moment one member becomes a person the reader starts measuring the threat by that person instead of by the size of the thing behind them.

How to apply it

  1. Decide which kind of group this is before writing a member: a cast of antagonists the reader will name, or a faceless collective whose whole job is scale. The two are built differently and the choice does not survive being made halfway through.
  2. Write the group's purpose in one sentence a reader could repeat back after a single scene, and check every member's behaviour against it, so the unit is established before any individual is.
  3. Count the scenes the group actually has, then set the roster to that, not to the size the organisation would realistically be.
  4. Give each member one axis that is theirs alone and cannot be traded: Fullmetal Alchemist hands each of its Homunculi a name from the deadly sins and then makes the method match the sin, and Bleach gives each Espada a different aspect of death to embody.
  5. Put the whole roster in one room on the page and cut any member who could not be picked out of that room without their power being named.
  6. If the group is the faceless kind, keep the individuals out on purpose: uniform behaviour, uniform speech, no member handed a private want, and any exception written as a deliberate break rather than the start of a cast.

The test

  1. Can you say what this group stands for in one sentence without naming a member?
  2. Is the roster bigger than the time you have to develop it?
  3. Take any member out of the group and describe them alone: is there anything there that no other member has?
  4. Could two members swap their dialogue in a scene without a reader noticing?
  5. If the group is meant to be faceless, has any member acquired a want, a voice or a face the reader could hold on to, and was that break deliberate?

Failure mode

A reader finishes the arc able to name the leader and maybe a couple of the others, and cannot say who the rest were or what any of them wanted. The group reads as strong on the page and hollow in memory. The clearest tell is a reader describing a member by their power alone, because the power was the only thing the story ever established about them. The second tell is that arrival scenes stop landing: each new member is met with recognition of a type rather than curiosity about a person, so the group's later reveals have nothing to land on.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict
S-00301:14-03:37Rule one is individuality: a legendary villain organisation stands for something and feels like a unified force while every member feels like their own person. Worked through Hunter x Hunter's Phantom Troupe as a room of unmistakable individuals, Fullmetal Alchemist's Homunculi as figures built to carry one goal and each defined by a named deadly sin, and Bleach's Espada, the top-ranked Arrancar in Aizen's forces, each built around a different aspect of deathsupports
S-00303:38-05:37The counter-example from the same series: Bleach's Sternritter, a larger roster than the Espada, each granted a letter of the alphabet and an ability tied to it, are argued to arrive, show a wild power and vanish, so that by the end of the arc the presenter says he could not tell you who most of them are. He puts the thinning down to a shortened runway forced on the authorsupports (counter-example)
S-00305:38-07:13The deliberate exception: not every villain organisation needs a developed roster, and which it needs depends on the role that organisation plays in the story. Invincible's Viltrumites and Blame!'s Silicon Life are offered as groups whose weight is their scale, where the presenter argues that giving every member a distinct identity would destroy the feeling the story wants; he names sci-fi as the home of this designsupports

Disagreements

None yet.

P-006 Every villain leaves a mark

Every member of a villain group changes the heroes' story in a way no other member does; if two members leave the same mark, one of them is a wall, not a villain.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

A villain group earns its place by what it does to the heroes, one member at a time, and each member must change the heroes' story in a way that is theirs alone: a wound, a betrayal, a question a hero cannot answer, a truth that rewrites what a hero thought had happened. The test is subtraction. Cut a member out and something in the heroes' story has to go missing with them; if the story closes over the gap unchanged, that member was scenery with a name and a power. Two members who leave the same mark are one villain and one wall, and the fix is to cut one or give one a different job, never to make the second one louder or stronger. Where P-005 caps how many members a group can carry, this rule decides what each of those members is for.

Why it works

An audience does not track a roster, it tracks changes to the people it cares about. A member whose only function is to be harder than the last one is measured on a single axis, so the audience knows what he is for before he opens his mouth and the only live question is how long he takes to fall. A member who leaves his own mark makes the heroes' future different, so the shape of the story cannot be predicted from the fight. Distinct marks also make the group larger than the sum of its members: the heroes come out of the arc carrying several different injuries, and the group becomes the one thing that did all of them. And the rule defends the roster from padding, because a writer who cannot name a member's mark has just been told that member is not needed yet.

How to apply it

  1. Write one line per member: the single change this member makes to a hero that no other member makes. If the line will not come, the member is not ready to be written.
  2. Name whose story each mark lands on. A mark on "the heroes" in general is not a mark.
  3. Cut or merge any member whose line duplicates another's, merging into one stronger character rather than keeping both.
  4. Vary the kind of mark, not only its size: a personal loss, a belief put under attack, a revelation that reframes the past, a betrayal that changes the shape of the plot.
  5. Run the subtraction test on every member before drafting their arc: delete them and read the heroes' story back. Whatever goes missing is their mark.
  6. Give the walls a job instead of a promotion. An obstacle that exists to be beaten is fine as staging, as long as it is not sold to the audience as a member of the group.

The test

  1. Can you state in one sentence the change this member makes to a named hero?
  2. Is that change different in kind from every other member's, rather than the same change at a higher intensity?
  3. If this member were deleted, would something in the heroes' story be missing?
  4. Do the marks land on different heroes, or on different parts of one hero, rather than all on the same nerve?
  5. Read the members' arcs in order: does each one end somewhere the previous one did not?

Failure mode

Every appearance runs the same beat in the same order: the sympathetic past, the fight that pushes the heroes past their limit, the defeat. Members can each be a good villain and the group can still fail this test, because the failure is structural rather than individual: no single member is badly written, and the repetition is in the pattern they are all cut to. A reader notices it as flattening. By the third or fourth arrival they can predict the shape of the arc from the member's first appearance and be right every time, so they stop asking what this one will cost the heroes and start asking only how strong he is.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict
S-00308:39-11:28The model: a squad of rogue ninja hunting the world's most powerful creatures for a plan to reshape it, whose members each change the story differently. Itachi is presented as the killer of his own clan, which drives Sasuke's revenge across hundreds of chapters until the reveal that he acted to stop a coup turns Sasuke against his own village instead. Pain destroys that village and kills people close to Naruto, then puts the cycle of hatred to him as an argument. Zetsu is secretly serving the final boss and betrays the group. The presenter's own claim, that no member is doing the same thing as another, is carried as his argumentsupports
S-00311:29-13:40The counter-example: the ranked demons serving Muzan, whom the presenter rates individually as good villains with memorable personalities and backstories, and whose contribution he argues is the same every time, a tragic past, the heroes pushed past their limits, defeat. His reading is carried as his. It is also weaker than he states it: one of the demons he lists as another tragic past was a criminal in his human life, so the pattern he names breaks on his own examplesupports (counter-example)

Disagreements

None yet.

P-007 A group you can see

The eye reads a group as a unit before the plot does, through a shared uniform, symbol or palette that each member's own silhouette still stands out inside; drop the shared mark and the group stops existing on screen.

Contents

  1. Rule
  2. Why it works
  3. How to apply it
  4. The test
  5. Failure mode
  6. Evidence
  7. Disagreements
  8. In Hood Ninjaz

Rule

A villain group has to be legible to the eye before it is legible to the plot, and that takes two things at once. One shared mark, a uniform, a symbol or a colour palette, carried by every member who appears on screen, so a reader meeting them for the first time files them as one force without being told. And one silhouette per member that the shared mark cannot swallow, so the group reads as a set of people rather than a rank of copies. Where the story genuinely forbids a uniform, the shared mark shrinks to something small and private and the silhouettes carry the rest of the load; it is not deleted. A group whose shared mark goes away, with nothing designed to replace it, stops existing on screen no matter what the script keeps calling it.

Why it works

A reader sorts a frame into sides before reading a word of it, so the design is the first statement the story makes about who these people are to each other. The shared mark does the group half of that job, converting a list of characters into one body with one intent. The distinct silhouette does the individual half, keeping each member recoverable inside the uniform so a reader can hold them apart in memory between appearances. The two halves are in tension by design: too much unity and the members blur into wallpaper, too much individuality and the organisation dissolves into a coincidence of people. This is not P-001 at group scale, and the two should not be run together: P-001 is the image of one power tied to one character, and this page is the image of one group, so every member can pass P-001 while the group still fails here. What makes the shared mark expensive to abandon is that it does its work cumulatively: the audience spends a whole series learning to read it, and once learned, the mark alone is enough to raise the threat before a single member is named.

How to apply it

  1. Choose one shared mark for the group and own it exclusively: a garment, a symbol or a palette that no other faction in the story uses.
  2. Put the mark in every scene the group appears in, including the scenes where only one member turns up, so it stays the group's signature rather than a meeting-room costume.
  3. Give each member one feature at silhouette level, not colour level, that the shared mark cannot hide: build, headgear, a carried weapon, a body that is not human standard. Naruto's Akatsuki are the model, with the black robe with red clouds sitting over a shark-like body, a scorpion-like puppet shell, a scythe carried on an extendable rope, and a nonhuman member built on plant biology.
  4. Draw the roster as filled black shapes at thumbnail size and check no two match. If two match, change a silhouette, never a colour.
  5. If the group must work unseen, shrink the mark instead of deleting it, and raise the silhouette work to carry what the uniform no longer carries. The source offered the Phantom Troupe as its example of a covert group and the fact check refuted that: the Troupe are feared worldwide, so they are not covert, but they do show the shrunken-mark form, one shared tattoo in place of a uniform.
  6. If the shared mark is ever retired inside the story, design its replacement in the same beat, and check the replacement is a mark and not just a plot reason for its absence.
  7. Keep every member's design in one visual language. New members join the language rather than importing their own.

The test

  1. In a frame with no dialogue and no name captions, would a first-time reader place these characters as one organisation?
  2. Is there a single shared mark, and does every member who appears on screen carry it?
  3. At thumbnail size, in filled black shapes only, can you tell every member apart?
  4. Does each member keep one feature the shared mark cannot hide?
  5. If the group works unseen and wears no uniform, is there still one small shared mark, and are the silhouettes doing more work because of it?
  6. If the shared mark is dropped at any point in the story, does something else take over its job in the same beat?
  7. Does every design in the group belong to the same visual language as the others?

Failure mode

It arrives in two steps and the second one is what a reader actually notices. First the unifier goes, whether by plot reason or by drift, so nothing in a frame says these people are together. Then the individual designs, no longer being held in line by anything shared, start pulling against each other, and the reader's eye finds no place to rest and no way to file who is who. The symptom reads as a set of characters the script keeps calling an organisation while they never once look like one, and a reader who could name every member of the earlier group cannot reliably name two of this one. S-003 reads Boruto's Kara this way: its Inner members are documented as wearing dark cloaks with Roman-numeral facial tattoos and their own cloak linings, and the presenter's account is that the cloaks stop appearing and the remaining designs, mixing sci-fi body modification with clashing period and costume references, no longer read as one organisation. The dropping of the cloaks is his reading of the later art, not a recorded change.

Evidence

SourceTimestampsWhat it arguedVerdict
S-00313:41-16:45The third test is visual design: a villain group needs a shared uniform, symbol or palette, with an exception claimed for groups working in the shadows. Naruto's Akatsuki are offered as the model, the black robe with red clouds unifying members whose own silhouettes stay distinctsupports (the claim that every member wears the robe is corrected on the source page)
S-00316:46-19:11Boruto's Kara is the counter-example: the presenter argues the group's cloaks stop appearing and the designs left behind clash, so the members no longer read as one organisation the way the Akatsuki still dosupports (counter-example)

Disagreements

None yet.

Rows 15, 18 and 20 are graded SECONDARY rather than FAN-WIKI-UNFETCHED: none of the three URLs (screenrant, cbr, gamerant) is a fan wiki, and row 18 is P-003's sole support for Deku's early bone-breaking, which lint check 6 forbids resting on a FAN-WIKI-UNFETCHED fact alone. Declared here so the divergence from a plain fan-wiki default is a stated call, not a silent one.

Body

Pavelover opens by naming the pattern he wants to explain: a protagonist's early powers are weak and expensive to use, yet those fights feel epic, while the same character's later, flawless powers feel boring (00:00). His first case is Rock Lee, the one Naruto ninja with no ninjutsu or genjutsu, "the only thing he can do is throw hands" (00:36), set against the Eight Gates, a system of body points that grant enormous speed and power at an escalating physical cost. Facing Gaara's automatic sand defence, Lee opens the gates and, in Pavelover's words, "this technique is literally destroying his body with every single move" (01:31).

On screen

A title card reads "THE EIGHT GATES" over a desaturated, distorted close-up of a screaming face, the essay's own naming of the technique.

His second case is Yuji Itadori's Divergent Fist against Mahito in Jujutsu Kaisen, introduced by contrast with Gojo, named in passing as having "one of the most busted abilities in fiction" (02:01). Divergent Fist, Pavelover notes, is not a real technique at all, but "just a side effect of not being able to properly control this aura" (02:24), pitted against a shapeshifting opponent who can remake his own and others' bodies. His third case is Ichigo Kurosaki's inner Hollow in Bleach: a power that, if it takes over, "could hurt the people he's actually trying to protect" (02:57), forcing Ichigo to hold back rather than commit fully.

On screen

A hollow, fanged mask over orange hair with a red energy trail, matched to the "overpowered" and "brutal" framing of an uncontrolled transformation.

Pavelover then names the pattern across all three: "Lee is dealing with physical drawbacks. Yuji is stuck with these super limited abilities and Ichigo has these mental barriers" (03:49), three different kinds of cost producing the same effect, forcing creativity rather than brute force.

After a sponsor break, he turns to a deliberately chosen counter-example: Sung Jin-Woo in Solo Leveling. Jin-Woo starts as, in his words, the "weakest dude ever" (05:32). Pavelover walks through three fights spread over Season 2, an ice boss (the "no damage taken" specifics here are UNVERIFIABLE, see Verification row 14), a spell-casting orc, and the character Beru, each won "without taking any damage" (06:53), though on the Beru fight he immediately qualifies himself, admitting he is exaggerating and that "Jin-Woo's abilities just prevent him from getting poisoned." Pavelover's read is that every ability works exactly as it should with no persisting drawback, which is why "it feels boring to see him win because you know he's going to win" (08:04).

On screen

A purple skill-notification card reads "[Active Skill] Mutilation LV.MAX", the video's own visual of a costless power-up.

He then pairs Jin-Woo directly against Deku in My Hero Academia, a second character who also "started as a complete weakling" (08:34). One For All initially breaks Deku's own bones on use; his fight against Todoroki has him breaking his own finger "again and again" for a shockwave attack "until his entire arm is completely messed up" (09:44). Pavelover's stated variable is time (11:07), a comparison the fact check holds as UNVERIFIABLE (see Verification row 21; the figure itself is held on the My Hero Academia case study).

His final model pairs One Piece and Naruto to argue that a drawback rotates rather than disappears. Luffy's Gear Second, Third, Fourth and Fifth each carry a distinct cost (exhaustion, shrinking, a Haki lockout, visible aging), and while some early costs ease as Luffy grows stronger, "there's always some kind of limitation holding him back" (13:32).

On screen

A close-up inflated fist stamped "GEAR THIRD," naming the ability mid-explanation.

He extends the same point to Naruto's later transformations, each carrying "a completely different cost" (13:47), and states the rule directly: the greatest series never simply remove a character's drawbacks as they grow stronger, and there is always a price to pay, only "they change them" (14:14). He closes by returning to Gojo as the apparent exception, arguing even he is "at their absolute coolest when you see the flaws in their powers" (14:38), before restating the thesis: "the best abilities are the ones that force characters to make sacrifices" (14:52).

What the guide took from this

  1. P-004, Flaws that never go away: sharpened with the three cost types this source isolates (the body, as Rock Lee's Eight Gates; the ability's own limits, as Yuji's Divergent Fist; the mind, as Ichigo's inner Hollow) and the rotation pattern (One Piece's Gears, Naruto's later forms: each new form retires the old cost and carries a new one)
  2. P-003, Earned progression: strengthened with a second worked pairing, Deku against Todoroki, isolating time as the real variable, and a more granular breakdown of Solo Leveling's costless fight sequence
  3. CS-naruto: new case study, Rock Lee's Eight Gates and the Gaara fight as the model for a structural physical cost, plus Naruto's later forms for the rotation pattern
  4. CS-jujutsu-kaisen: new case study, Yuji's Divergent Fist against Mahito as the model for an ability-limitation cost, with Gojo held as the flawless-seeming aside and his Domain Expansion, already cited by S-001, carried onto the page
  5. CS-bleach: new case study, Ichigo's inner Hollow as the model for a mental control-barrier cost
  6. CS-my-hero-academia: new case study, Deku's One For All and the Todoroki fight as the paired model against Solo Leveling for P-003
  7. CS-one-piece: the Gomu Gomu no Mi to Hito Hito no Mi retcon and the detail that Gear Third's shrink eases with growth, both new facts this source adds
  8. CS-solo-leveling: the Red Gate ice fight (held as unverified, Verification row 14), the orc fight and Beru added as further, more granular evidence for the counter-example reading

What the guide did not take

  • Pavelover's episode-count comparison for Deku's peak against Jin-Woo's: "narrative peak" is not a defined, checkable event in either series, so the specific figures are held as UNVERIFIABLE (Verification row 21) rather than adopted as a fact on any page; the numbers themselves are held on the My Hero Academia case study, not restated here
  • The four opinion claims (Verification rows 5, 6, 9, 28): the presenter's judgments that specific fights are "the greatest in anime history" and character-quality claims about Gojo, none of which are checkable facts
  • The FlexiSpot sponsor read (roughly 04:29-05:32 and 15:03-15:30) and the outro tease: not argument content
  • The candidate fifth principle: this source's central move (how long a drawback is sustained relative to a series' total length, and whether the same drawback rotates onto the next power tier) is folded into P-004 as an extension rather than spun out as a new page; PROTOCOL.md's stop rule caps principle pages by novelty, and this reading sharpens an existing rule rather than adding a new one
  • Two deep-pass frames that could not be confidently matched to a named character at the available resolution (a light-haired face at 02:15, a golden winged silhouette at 10:51): held as unidentified rather than asserted

Body

Pavelover opens by asking why some protagonist powers are unforgettable and others are not, and says he will compare three protagonists to find out (00:00). His first element is what he calls iconic powers, "the special sauce that will make these powers instantly recognizable" (00:25). His first case is Luffy: a plain rubber body that, he argues, becomes distinct through how it is used, "this simple rubber power is completely different from every other stretchy character out there," with attacks that are "often improvised and really creative" (00:51).

On screen

A caption card reads "WHAT'S THE SECRET SAUCE HERE?" over a Luffy trading-card-style graphic, the video's own recurring refrain for this question.

His second iconic case is Rudo from Gachiakuta, a character who turns discarded objects, a wooden box, a keyboard, a toilet plunger, into weapons. Pavelover frames the appeal as visual distinctness: "when a protagonist's power has a visually distinct look and feels different from the generic stuff that everyone else is doing, it can become instantly iconic" (01:35). He adds that a power can also become iconic through a signature move, naming "the Kamehameha or Gojo's Domain Expansion" as examples (01:35).

On screen

A full character card names Rudo directly against a dark red background, and later a Fandom-style wiki card gives Gachiakuta's own publication history.

His contrast case is Jinwoo from Solo Leveling, whom he frames as the negative: dagger skills and shadow soldiers that, in his words, "already exist in a bunch of other anime and manga," so that "Jinwoo's powers feel more like he grabbed a bunch of different abilities from a bunch of different places instead of having one thing that's really unique to him" (02:37).

The second element is that abilities need to be simple. His argument for Luffy: one ability, stretching, solves three unrelated problems (a sand opponent, an electricity opponent, a mochi opponent) through creative reapplication rather than new tools, and "my man manages to beat all of them using nothing but his ability to stretch his body" (03:46). He extends the same reading to Rudo, whose single transformation mechanic still varies by "how Rudo interprets the object" (04:50), and contrasts both with Jinwoo's fight, which he lists as a long chain of distinct abilities used one after another, concluding "when you have everything, nothing feels special" (06:06).

The third element is that progression should feel earned. Pavelover says Rudo's progression is hard to judge because Gachiakuta "hasn't been out for that long" (06:45), then walks through Luffy's Gear forms as a counter-model: Gear Second arrives at an emotionally weighted point in the story, deep into the manga and anime by the on-screen title cards quoted below (see the One Piece case study for the confirmed count); Gear Fourth follows "after several years of training"; Gear Fifth follows "after he literally died" (06:45).

On screen

Title cards read "387 CHAPTERS" and, moments later, "272 ANIME EPISODES," each over a blurred background of the relevant material.

He sets this against Jinwoo, who goes "from weakest hunter to a literal god tier character in like 20 episodes," which he reads as a skipped journey rather than an earned one (08:35).

On screen

A "SEASON 1" versus "SEASON 2" comparison graphic, with a dashed progress line, illustrates the pacing claim.

The fourth element is that abilities need flaws that persist. He opens with a basketball analogy: a shooter who tires after every shot is more compelling to watch than one who never misses, because "powers without drawbacks are boring" (10:16). Applied to Luffy: he can't swim, Gear Second costs him visible strain, Gear Third leaves him briefly shrunk, Gear Fourth costs him his Haki, Gear Fifth visibly ages him, and Pavelover's point is that "there's always something holding him back from becoming completely overpowered" (11:00). Rudo's power is capped to three activations (per the video's stated wording, corrected on the source's wiki, see Verification row 13) and objects that must already be discarded. Jinwoo, by contrast, is given a chain of fallback options, mana potion, melee, ranged magic, invisibility, that the video reads as never really failing him, closing on the line "when these abilities have no meaningful limitations, fights just become boring" (13:13).

He closes: "great powers are the difference between an average and an amazing protagonist" (13:45), before a short outro tease for an unrelated video on character uniqueness and worldbuilding, which carries no further argument content for this source.

What the guide took from this

  1. P-001, Iconic powers: built from Luffy (a familiar idea made strange) and Rudo (a visually distinct mechanic with no precedent in the comparison set), with Jinwoo held up as the negative case
  2. P-002, Simple powers: built from Luffy's and Rudo's single-mechanism reapplication, against Jinwoo's list-of-abilities approach
  3. P-003, Earned progression: built from the Gear Second/Fourth/Fifth escalation against Jinwoo's compressed rise
  4. P-004, Flaws that never go away: built from the Gear-form drawbacks and Rudo's activation limits, against Jinwoo's chain of fallback options
  5. CS-one-piece: the model case across all four principles
  6. CS-gachiakuta: a second model case for P-001, with supporting evidence for P-002 and P-004; the video's own caveat that its progression can't yet be judged is carried onto that page
  7. CS-solo-leveling: the counter-example across all four principles

What the guide did not take

  • The specific identity of the two unidentified One Piece antagonists at 00:04:09 and 00:04:42 in the evidence file: the fact check confirms which three opponents the "sand, electricity, mochi" line describes (Crocodile, Enel, Katakuri) but not which deep-pass frame shows which fighter. Held as unverified on the One Piece case study rather than asserted
  • The presenter's basketball framing and the specific analogy shots (a Kuroko no Basuke court, a Haikyuu volleyball cutaway): illustration, not an argument claim
  • The unidentified hook-montage clips at 00:00:45 and 00:00:57, which could not be identified by source anime at the available resolution
  • Gachiakuta's serialization date, English-language license, and anime-adaptation announcement (from the on-screen wiki card): on-screen only, not run through the fact-check pass, held as unverified on the Gachiakuta case study rather than asserted as fact
  • The presenter's rhetorical asides ("secret sauce," the crown prop, the outro tease for a different video): style, not a transferable rule

See also

Gachiakuta, One Piece, Solo Leveling, P-001 Iconic powers, P-002 Simple powers, P-003 Earned progression, P-004 Flaws that never go away

Pavelover - How To Write A Villain Group (& How NOT To)

the three villain-group tests (individuality, purpose, visual design), each proved by pairing a work that passes it with one that fails it

Segment map

#TimeWhat it arguesFeeds
100:00-01:13Thesis: a villain group either outshines the heroes or is forgotten the moment the show ends, and rules decide which-
201:14-03:37Rule one, individuality: the group reads as one force while every member stands on their own. Models: Phantom Troupe, Homunculi, EspadaP-005, CS-hunter-x-hunter, CS-fullmetal-alchemist, CS-bleach
303:38-05:37Counter-example, the Sternritter: the same setup on paper, a larger roster with less time, so the members do not landP-005, CS-bleach
405:38-07:13The exception: a faceless collective is a deliberate choice, and individual identity would wreck it. Viltrumites, Silicon LifeP-005, CS-invincible, CS-blame
507:14-08:39Sponsor read, cut-
608:39-11:28Rule two, purpose: every member leaves a mark no other member could leave. Model: the Akatsuki, through Itachi, Pain and ZetsuP-006, CS-naruto
711:29-13:40Counter-example, the Twelve Kizuki: individually strong, structurally identical, so each contributes the same beatP-006, CS-demon-slayer
813:41-16:45Rule three, visual design: a shared mark the eye reads as a unit, with distinct silhouettes inside it. Model: the Akatsuki cloakP-007, CS-naruto
916:46-19:11Counter-example, Kara: the shared capes are dropped, then the designs clash, so the group stops existing on screenP-007, CS-boruto, CS-naruto
1019:12-19:27Conclusion: the best villain organisations make part of you want them on screen while you root for the heroes-
1119:27-19:36Silent end card, cut-

Corrections

Auto-caption mis-hearings, with the basis for each correction. The captions on this source are unusually accurate: every anime, character and studio name matched an on-screen card or the published franchise spelling, with one exception.

TimestampHeard (auto-caption)Corrected
04:04"Yuhabaha"Yhwach (Bleach's Quincy emperor and the Sternritter's leader). The captions transliterate the Japanese pronunciation rather than the published romanisation. The Sternritter name cards at 04:17-04:24 confirm the arc. This corrects the caption spelling only, not the speaker

Three further caption passages are garbled and are flagged unquotable rather than corrected, because the intended words cannot be recovered from the captions or the frames: 06:21 ("Another legendary poll is the Silicon Life from Blame!"), 08:43 ("every member feels the stake", probably "distinct", but that is a guess) and 14:02 ("You don't have no types of drip, swag, swaggerty", probably a deliberate joke delivery). The sense of each is clear; the wording is not, so none of the three is quoted anywhere on this page. Two on-screen spellings are the editor's errors and not the speaker's: every Espada card reads EPSADA, and the Blame! card reads SILICONE LIFE where the captions say Silicon Life.

Twenty-six further observations from the frame pass were dropped from scope as pure editing detail (logo cards, sound-effect lettering, sponsor overlays, the on-camera hat gag, the STOP-overlay running joke): they describe how the video is cut rather than what it argues about a work, and none of them changes a claim.

Body

Pavelover opens on two states a villain group can end in: the roster you finish a series thinking is cooler than the heroes, and the roster whose members you forget the moment the show is over. His framing question is what separates them. His answer, offered explicitly as opinion, is three rules, and each rule is proved the same way, by setting a work that passes it against a work that fails it.

On screen

"RULE 1 / INDIVIDUALITY"

Rule one is the group itself. His argument is that a legendary villain organisation reads as one unified force with a clear identity while every member inside it still reads as a person. Three models carry it. The Phantom Troupe in Hunter x Hunter: "if you put all of them in a room, every single one of them feels completely different" (01:41), and he lists the clown, the woman with the living vacuum cleaner and the mummy. The Homunculi in Fullmetal Alchemist, built for one goal and each defined by a different deadly sin, Lust manipulating, Wrath calculating, Gluttony eating the evidence. And, his stated favourite, the Espada in Bleach, ranked under Aizen, each embodying a different aspect of death, and that aspect running through how each one talks, carries himself and fights.

On screen

Six name cards run from about 03:02 to 03:11, one or two seconds each: "EPSADA NO. 06 / DESTRUCTION / Barragan Luisenbarn", "EPSADA NO. 04 / EMPTINESS / Ulquiorra Cifer", "EPSADA NO. 02 / OLD AGE / Barragan Luisenbarn", "EPSADA NO. 08 / MADNESS / Szayelaporro Granz", "EPSADA NO. 03 / SACRIFICE / Tier Harribel", "EPSADA NO. 10 / RAGE / Yammy Llargo". Every card misspells the group. The first names the wrong character: the narration over it says Grimmjow is destruction, and the narration is the half that is canon-correct (Verification rows 25, 29 to 32, 159 to 163).

He closes the rule on the shape it produces: the organisation feels like a unit while every member stands on their own two feet, and "nobody is interchangeable or just the other guy in the group" (03:33).

The counter-example is Bleach's own second organisation. On paper the Sternritter have the identical setup, an elite army under the series' final antagonist, each member issued a letter and a supernatural ability to match.

On screen

Four name cards run 04:17 to 04:24: "THE H / HEAT / Bazzard Black", "THE I / IRON / Cang Du", "THE A / ANTITHESIS / Uryu Ishida", "THE E / EXPLODE / Bambietta Basterbine".

His complaint is what the roster does with them: most "show up, demonstrate some wild imaginative power while fighting one of the heroes, and then they are gone" (04:33) before anything else about them lands. He points at three members he does not name, dismisses each in a sentence, and the editor overrides him.

On screen

A tape-style STOP indicator drops in at about 04:58 with yellow text reading "*What Pave ment to say was: 'BazzB Is an amazing character, and the goat.'" The same gag returns over the Demon Slayer section.

Then he gives the mechanism rather than the taste: more members, less time each. He also gives a real-world cause rather than a judgement.

On screen

A text block at about 05:20 states that Kubo's declining health forced him to end the manga, cites "the 2017 Giga Jump Interview" as a green underlined hyperlink, and quotes him on leaning his body weight on his left shoulder while drawing and being told there was a partial fracture. The card's own name for the magazine is reversed, and the quote's provenance is unconfirmed (Verification rows 50, 171).

He then qualifies the whole rule, and this is the part a writer needs most. Not every villain organisation needs developed individuals: it depends on the role the group plays. His case is Invincible's Viltrumites, which he describes as a species raised identically into weapons so the group reads as an unstoppable force (canon gives a eugenics programme instead, Verification row 56), and Blame!'s Silicon Life is his second, where "the emphasis is never really placed on any single member" (06:37) but on the scale of the collective. Giving those groups distinct identities, he argues, "would actually kind of destroy the whole entire feeling they're going for" (06:50). He names Cyberpunk, Ghost in the Shell and Blame! as the genre that does this best, and offers no argument for the first two.

The sponsor read is cut here. It resumes at 08:39.

On screen

"RULE 2 / PURPOSE"

Rule two is what each member does to the story. "Every single member needs to leave their own mark on the journey of the good guys" (09:01). He names the Disaster Curses and Claw in passing, then spends the section on the Akatsuki, and works three members to show three different marks. Itachi is presented for most of the series as the killer of his own clan, which is the engine of Sasuke's entire arc, until the reveal turns the character inside out and sends Sasuke down a different path.

On screen

A white overlay reading "393 Chapters / 388 Episodes" holds from about 09:57 to 10:02 across nine different shots. The chapter figure matches the chapter where the Sasuke and Itachi fight ends; the episode figure matches no documented milestone and is the video's own count (Verification rows 84, 183).

Pain does something else entirely: he destroys the village, kills people Naruto loved, and forces Naruto to decide whether he is feeding the cycle of hatred. Zetsu does a third thing, betraying the organisation from inside for the story's final antagonist. The rule falls out of the pattern: "not a single one of these guys is doing the same thing", and "if you pulled one member out of this organization, you would be ripping out an entire piece of the story itself" (11:07, 11:11).

The counter-example is Demon Slayer, and he is careful to praise the members before attacking the structure. Rui, Gyutaro and Hantengu each get a backstory, an escalating fight and a defeat, and he runs the same three beats three times in the same words to make the point audible: increasingly difficult challenge, heroes show their growth, tragic reminder of lost humanity. His verdict is not that the demons are bad but that each contributes the same thing, so most of them are "just the next obstacle, the next wall the heroes have to break through" (13:31). The rule he draws from it: every new member should make the audience feel something nobody else in the roster could have given them.

On screen

"RULE 3 / VISUAL DESIGN"

Rule three is the eye. His argument is that people judge on sight before they judge on writing, and that fiction has exploited this for a long time: the four nations in Avatar, the houses in Harry Potter, Team Rocket. What is needed is "a shared uniform, a recurring symbol, a color palette, there has to be something that ties everybody together and tells your brain that these people are a unit" (14:37). He then names the exception in the same breath: groups trying to operate in the shadows do not want to look connected, and he offers the Phantom Troupe, Night Raid and Claw. The model is the Akatsuki cloak, and his reading is that its power is simplicity, a discreet black robe whose red clouds do all the signalling, and that the silhouettes underneath stay wildly different, Zetsu, Kisame, Hidan and Sasori inside one uniform, "every single one is physically different, yet instantly recognizable" (16:08).

The final counter-example is Boruto's Kara. He credits the handover fairly first, naming Ikemoto as Kishimoto's long-time assistant and calling the job an incredibly difficult one, before making the design case: Kara starts in dark capes, which at least tries to unite them visually, and then "they just stop wearing them" (17:45), at which point the one thing holding the group's visual identity together stops existing. What is left, he argues, is a set of individually busy designs that do not read as one organisation. He closes on the thesis: the best villain organisations are written and designed so well that part of you cannot wait for the villains to be on screen, even while you are rooting for the heroes.

What the guide took from this

  1. P-005, A group of individuals: the new principle this source founds, built on its individuality section. What it supplied is three worked models, one counter-example inside the same franchise, the roster-size mechanism (more members against less time each) and the faceless-collective exception the presenter insists is a choice rather than a default
  2. P-006, Every villain leaves a mark: the new principle from the purpose section. What it supplied is the Akatsuki's three worked members, each altering the heroes' story differently, set against a counter-example whose members repeat one structural beat
  3. P-007, A group you can see: the new principle from the visual-design section. What it supplied is the Akatsuki cloak worked in detail, the Kara counter-example, and the stated exception for groups operating in the shadows. Related to P-001 by scale and deliberately kept separate: P-001 is the image of one power, P-007 the image of one group
  4. CS-hunter-x-hunter: new case study, the Phantom Troupe as a model for P-005, and as his lead example of the shadows exception under P-007, an example the fact check corrects (row 119)
  5. CS-fullmetal-alchemist: new case study, the Homunculi as a model for P-005, one goal and seven distinct definitions
  6. CS-bleach: updated, the Espada as a model for P-005 and the Sternritter as the counter-example, with the roster-size and shortened-runway evidence
  7. CS-demon-slayer: new case study, the Twelve Kizuki as the counter-example for P-006, individually strong and structurally identical
  8. CS-naruto: updated, the Akatsuki as the model for both P-006 (Itachi, Pain, Zetsu) and P-007 (the cloak, and the distinct silhouettes inside it)
  9. CS-boruto: new case study, Kara as the counter-example for P-007, the uniform he says is dropped (unverified, row 140) and the clashing designs
  10. CS-invincible: new case study, the Viltrumites as the faceless-collective exception under P-005
  11. CS-blame: new case study, the Silicon Life as the second exception under P-005, carried with the corrections the fact check forced

What the guide did not take

  • The works named without argument: Jujutsu Kaisen's Disaster Curses, Mob Psycho 100's Claw, Cyberpunk, Ghost in the Shell, Avatar's four nations, Harry Potter's houses, Pokemon's Team Rocket, Fire Force's White Clad, One Piece's CP9 and Akame ga Kill's Night Raid. Each is a name in a list, so none earns a case-study page under the stop rules; the visual examples that are only illustration (X-Men, Kuroko's Basketball, Despicable Me, The Lion King, SpongeBob SquarePants) are held on this page and nowhere else
  • The opinions and the taste, which is most of the video by row count: the superlatives about the Espada, Aizen, the Akatsuki and the worst plot twist in anime, and the aesthetic verdicts on Kara's designs. They are recorded as rows so a later reader can see what was excluded, never restated as fact
  • The two claims that failed verification outright: that Kara's members stop wearing the capes (row 140), which no source records as a change and which is the presenter's reading of the later art, and the unnamed Kara member with the flowy white top (row 147), who cannot be identified from this source at all. Both matter, because the dropped cape is load-bearing for his Kara argument
  • The Blame! rows whose values the check could not hold: the Silicon Life did not evolve separately from humans (row 58), and they are not competing to take control but hunting to stop control being regained (row 59), while the never-a-single-member absolute is loose (row 60). The exception P-005 records therefore rests on the presenter's craft reading, not on his description of the work, and the corrected version lives on the case-study page
  • The Viltrumite mechanism as he states it (row 56): canon gives a eugenics programme and a purge, not an identical upbringing, so the principle carries the effect and not his cause
  • The Reverse: 1999 sponsor read (07:14 to 08:39), including its CHECK THE LINK overlay: not argument content. Its nine claims were checked anyway and are kept in the table so the block is auditable rather than silently missing
  • The unquotable caption passages at 06:21, 08:43 and 14:02, and the 2017 interview quote's provenance (row 50), which stands as the card's claim only

See also

Blame, Bleach, Boruto, Demon Slayer, Fullmetal Alchemist, Hunter x Hunter, Invincible, Naruto, P-001 Iconic powers, P-005 A group of individuals, P-006 Every villain leaves a mark, P-007 A group you can see

Blame

the second model for the faceless-collective exception in P-005, through the Silicon Life

Contents

  1. Why it is in this library
  2. The power system in one paragraph
  3. Verified facts
  4. What the guide learned from it
  5. Contested or unverified
  6. Corrections to the sources

Why it is in this library

The work the source reaches for when a villain force is deliberately not a cast of people: the Silicon Life are written as a species, and the reader is never asked to pick a member out of a line-up.
It is also the weakest-sourced work in this intake, so this page carries only what survived the fact check.

The power system in one paragraph

Blame! has no power system in the shonen sense, so what matters for this library is the shape of the organisation. The Silicon Life are an artificial cyborg species: Wikipedia's character list files them as Silicon Life (Cyborgs), and the Order breeds humans without Net Terminal Genes to make more of them, so they did not evolve separately from humans. They are a species-wide force in a conflict over control of the City, spread across many Strata and organised into war bands under generals, inside a Megastructure whose radius is measured in astronomical units rather than a city measured in light years. Their goal runs the opposite way to a bid for the throne: they hunt carriers of the Net Terminal Genes to stop the Administration ever regaining control, because that control would mean their extinction, and the fan wiki records all but a few exceptions sharing that single goal.

Verified facts

FactVerdictSource tierReferenceCited by
The Silicon Life are an artificial cyborg species, filed by Wikipedia's character list as Silicon Life (Cyborgs), with the Order breeding humans without Net Terminal Genes to make more of them; they did not evolve separately from humansCORRECTEDSECONDARYhttps://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Blame!_charactersP-005
The setting is the Megastructure, radius 5.3 astronomical units, not a city measured in light yearsCORRECTEDSECONDARYhttps://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Blame!_charactersP-005
The Silicon Life are a species-wide force in a conflict over control of the City, spread across many Strata with war bands and generalsCORRECTEDFAN-WIKI-FETCHEDhttps://blame.fandom.com/wiki/Silicon_LifeP-005
They hunt Net Terminal Gene carriers to stop the Administration ever regaining control, because that control would mean their extinction, and all but a few exceptions share that one goalCORRECTEDFAN-WIKI-FETCHEDhttps://blame.fandom.com/wiki/Silicon_LifeP-005
The source's on-screen card spells the group "SILICONE LIFE" while its caption track says Silicon Life; the card is the error, the franchise term is Silicon LifeVERIFIEDPRIMARYhttps://www.youtube.com/watch?v=n933HtgVd1US-003
The source names Blame! alongside Cyberpunk and Ghost in the Shell as its sci-fi examplesVERIFIEDPRIMARYhttps://www.youtube.com/watch?v=n933HtgVd1UP-005
Those three comparables are built onto the screen as posters that end up side by side, the BLAME! Master Edition volume 1 cover among themVERIFIEDPRIMARYhttps://www.youtube.com/watch?v=n933HtgVd1US-003

Note: no fact on this page rests on an unfetched fan wiki. The Silicon Life fandom page was read in full outside WebFetch, which is why it is filed FAN-WIKI-FETCHED rather than FAN-WIKI-UNFETCHED.

What the guide learned from it

  • P-005: the faceless collective is a legitimate exception, and this is what it looks like when it is chosen rather than defaulted into. The threat is a species with its own war bands and generals, not a roster of named antagonists, and the story is built so no member has to be memorable
  • P-005: the exception costs something. Because the force is a species, its motive has to carry the weight a character normally carries, and here the motive is survival: stop the Administration regaining control, because that control is extinction
  • P-005: the source's own examples are the sci-fi cluster, Cyberpunk and Ghost in the Shell alongside this work, which is a note on where the exception tends to live rather than an argument that it only works there

Contested or unverified

The source argues that the emphasis in Blame! is on the collective threat and its scale, never on a single member. That is a reading of narrative emphasis, not a checkable fact, so it is carried here as his argument and never promoted to a principle page. The absolute is loose in any case: the work does name individual Silicon Life, and one of them, Davine Lu Linvega, leads her own faction with her own arc.

Corrections to the sources

  • S-003 said the Silicon Life are an artificial species that evolved separately from humans in a post-apocalyptic sci-fi world. They are an artificial cyborg species, but the separate evolution is wrong: the Order breeds humans without Net Terminal Genes to make more of them. The setting is the Megastructure, not a city measured in light years.
  • S-003 said the Silicon Life are presented as a whole civilisation competing for control of the world. The species-wide scale is right; the direction is inverted. They hunt Net Terminal Gene carriers to stop the Administration ever regaining control, because that control would mean their extinction, not to seize control themselves. The "competing goals" wording is dropped: all but a few exceptions share one goal.

See also

P-005 A group of individuals, Pavelover - How To Write A Villain Group (& How NOT To)

Cited by

Anime Guide log, P-005 A group of individuals, Pavelover - How To Write A Villain Group (& How NOT To)

Bleach

the model case for P-004 (a mental control-barrier drawback), and for P-005 both the model (the Espada) and its own counter-example (the Sternritter)

Contents

  1. Why it is in this library
  2. The power system in one paragraph
  3. Verified facts
  4. What the guide learned from it
  5. Contested or unverified
  6. Corrections to the sources

Why it is in this library

S-002's third model case for a permanent drawback type, one that sits in the mind rather than the body or the ability itself.
S-003's double case: one work that both passes and fails the same test, the Espada built as individuals and the Sternritter built as a larger roster with less time each.

The power system in one paragraph

Ichigo Kurosaki can access the power of an inner Hollow, a spirit living inside him that, left uncontrolled, endangers the people he is trying to protect. Early in the series this forces him into a genuine mental struggle over whether to use the power at all, holding back during fights rather than committing fully.

The two villain organisations are structured in opposite ways. The Espada are the top-performing Arrancar, hand-picked by Aizen and ranked by number inside his forces, each one carrying a named aspect of death that shapes how that member looks, speaks and fights. The Sternritter are elite Quincy soldiers under Yhwach, each granted a letter of the alphabet, a Schrift, that names a supernatural ability; the roster is far larger, it arrives late in the series, and most of its members get a single appearance rather than a place in the story.

Note on tiers: the two P-004 rows about Ichigo's inner Hollow are both FAN-WIKI-UNFETCHED, so that load-bearing point rests entirely on unfetched fan wikis and may never be the sole basis of a craft rule. Every P-005 row above is PRIMARY or SECONDARY. The Bleach Fandom pages returned HTTP 402 and could not be read at all, which is why no Japanese term for Ulquiorra's aspect is carried here.

What the guide learned from it

  • P-004: Ichigo's inner Hollow is the source's clearest example of a permanent cost held in the character's own mind, a decision to hold back rather than a physical or mechanical limit, and one that produces disproportionate destructive force when it fully takes over
  • P-005: the Espada are the model. A roster small enough for each member to get screen time, with a named aspect of death per seat that gives every one of them a different bearing, speech and fighting style, is a group a reader can pick out of a line-up
  • P-005: the Sternritter are the counter-example, and the same author wrote both. A far larger roster arriving in the final arc gives most members one appearance, so the group reads as strong and stays faceless
  • P-005: the size gap is the whole comparison, ten seats against twenty-six letters, and the documented shortened runway for the final arc is the reason there was less time to spend on each member

Contested or unverified

Everything in this section is the presenter's assertion or opinion in S-003, carried as his and never promoted to a principle page.

  • That the Espada are his personal favourite example of a villain group.
  • That Aizen is one of the coldest antagonists to ever exist.
  • That the aspects of death bleed into each Espada's personality, speech, bearing and fighting.
  • That Bleach also shows what happens when individuality is not applied well.
  • That on a surface level the Sternritter are set up perfectly.
  • That most Sternritter show up, show a wild power, then are gone, and that the few who stand out are a handful out of the roster.
  • That by the end of the Sternritter arc a viewer could not say who half of them are. His dismissals of three of them, one as a scary guy there to help a character unlock a new power, one as an insane guy who can create things from his imagination, one as Grimmjow but worse and with a mohawk, are quoted as opinion only.
  • That the author rushed this part of the story. Rushed is his evaluative framing; the documented fact underneath it is the shortened runway, which sits in the table above.
  • The craft assertion that more members and less time to develop them gives a unit that feels strong but hollow. That is his argument, and it is what P-005 tests rather than something the fact check confirmed.
  • The provenance of the 2017 interview quote in the video's health graphic. The card exists and reads as claimed, but no fetchable reputable source carried the quote's wording, so it stands as the card's claim and not as independently verified.

Corrections to the sources

  • S-003 said the Espada are the ten most powerful Hollows in existence. They are the Arrancar numbered 1 to 10 inside Aizen's forces, his lieutenants, not a ranking across every Hollow that exists. Hollows is fair shorthand for Arrancar; the scope is not.
  • S-003 said every Sternritter gets a unique letter of the alphabet. The letters are not unique to one holder: Uryuu Ishida was granted A, the same letter as Yhwach; the twins Loyd and Royd Lloyd both hold Y; and a letter returns to Yhwach on its holder's death and can be redistributed.
  • Two slips inside S-003 itself, recorded so they are never re-adopted as facts about the work: every Espada card misspells the group as EPSADA, and the DESTRUCTION card names Barragan Luisenbarn when the narration over it correctly says Grimmjow. The health graphic also reverses the magazine's name, which is Jump Giga.

See also
